“我怎么办？喏，我坐下来想。我受过良好的古典教育，对一切营生又抱着实实在在的厌恶：这就是我闯世界的本钱。你知道吗，我听人把橄榄说成难吃的东西！何等可悲的非利士气！我常想，索尔兹伯里，在橄榄和红酒的作用下，我能写出真正的诗。来基安蒂吧；酒也许不怎么样，酒瓶可是实在迷人。”

“这儿的不坏。来一大瓶吧。”

“很好。于是我盘算我前景的匮乏，打定主意投身文学。”

“真的？那可怪了。你如今处境倒相当舒坦。”

===Original===

“Though! What a satire upon a noble profession. I am afraid, Salisbury, you haven’t a proper idea of the dignity of an artist. You see me sitting at my desk﻿—or at least you can see me if you care to call﻿—with pen and ink, and simple nothingness before me, and if you come again in a few hours you will (in all probability) find a creation!”

“Yes, quite so. I had an idea that literature was not remunerative.”

“You are mistaken; its rewards are great. I may mention, by the way, that shortly after you saw me I succeeded to a small income. An uncle died, and proved unexpectedly generous.”

“Ah, I see. That must have been convenient.”

“It was pleasant﻿—undeniably pleasant. I have always considered it in the light of an endowment of my researches. I told you I was a man of letters; it would, perhaps, be more correct to describe myself as a man of science.”

“Dear me, Dyson, you have really changed very much in the last few years. I had a notion, don’t you know, that you were a sort of idler about town, the kind of man one might meet on the north side of Piccadilly every day from May to July.”

“Exactly. I was even then forming myself, though all unconsciously. You know my poor father could not afford to send me to the University. I used to grumble in my ignorance at not having completed my education. That was the folly of youth, Salisbury; my University was Piccadilly. There I began to study the great science which still occupies me.”

“What science do you mean?”

“The science of the great city; the physiology of London; literally and metaphysically the greatest subject that the mind of man can conceive. What an admirable *salmi* this is; undoubtedly the final end of the pheasant. Yet I feel sometimes positively overwhelmed with the thought of the vastness and complexity of London. Paris a man may get to understand thoroughly with a reasonable amount of study; but London is always a mystery. In Paris you may say: ‘Here live the actresses, here the Bohemians, and the *Ratés*’; but it is different in London. You may point out a street, correctly enough, as the abode of washerwomen; but, in that second floor, a man may be studying Chaldee roots, and in the garret over the way a forgotten artist is dying by inches.”

===Chinese===

“倒！对一门高贵行业多大的讽刺。我看，索尔兹伯里，你对艺术家的尊严没有正确的观念。你瞧我坐在书桌前——你肯来串门就瞧得见——纸墨俱备，面前一片纯粹的虚无；过几个钟头你再来，你（十之八九）会见到一件创作！”

“是，正是。我原以为文学不挣钱。”

“你错了；它的报偿丰厚。顺便说一句，你见我不久之后，我承继了一小笔进项。一位叔父死了，死得出手阔绰，出人意料。”

“啊，明白了。那可真是来得方便。”

“是愉快——无可否认地愉快。我一向把它看作对我研究的一份资助。我说过我是文人；也许更准确地说，我是个科学中人。”

“哎呀，戴森，这几年你可真是大变了。我原先的印象，你知道，你是城里一个闲人，从五月到七月，天天能在皮卡迪利北边撞见的那种人。”

“正是。那时我就在塑造自己了，尽管全然不自觉。你知道，我可怜的父亲供不起我上大学。我从前无知，还抱怨学业没完成。那是年轻的愚蠢，索尔兹伯里；我的大学就是皮卡迪利。在那里，我开始研习至今占据着我的那门大科学。”

“什么科学？”

“大城的科学；伦敦的生理学；无论就字面还是就玄义，都是人的心智所能构想的最伟大的题目。这炖野味真是妙极；无疑是雉鸡的终极归宿。不过有时，一想到伦敦的浩大与繁复，我简直被压倒。巴黎，人下一番相当的功夫还能彻底弄懂；伦敦却永远是个谜。在巴黎你可以说：‘这里住女演员，这里住波希米亚人，还有落魄文人’；伦敦就不一样。你可以指认一条街，说它是洗衣妇的聚居地，分毫不差；可那二楼上，也许有个男人正在钻研迦勒底语的词根；对面阁楼里，一个被遗忘的画家正在一寸一寸地死去。”

===Original===

“I see you are Dyson, unchanged and unchangeable,” said Salisbury, slowly sipping his Chianti. “I think you are misled by a too fervid imagination; the mystery of London exists only in your fancy. It seems to me a dull place enough. We seldom hear of a really artistic crime in London, whereas I believe Paris abounds in that sort of thing.”

“Give me some more wine. Thanks. You are mistaken, my dear fellow, you are really mistaken. London has nothing to be ashamed of in the way of crime. Where we fail is for want of Homers, not Agamemnons. *Carent quia vate sacro*, you know.”

“I recall the quotation. But I don’t think I quite follow you.”

“Well, in plain language, we have no good writers in London who make a speciality of that kind of thing. Our common reporter is a dull dog; every story that he has to tell is spoilt in the telling. His idea of horror and of what excites horror is so lamentably deficient. Nothing will content the fellow but blood, vulgar red blood, and when he can get it he lays it on thick, and considers that he has produced a telling article. It’s a poor notion. And, by some curious fatality, it is the most commonplace and brutal murders which always attract the most attention and get written up the most. For instance, I dare say that you never heard of the Harlesden case?”

“No; no, I don’t remember anything about it.”

“Of course not. And yet the story is a curious one. I will tell it you over our coffee. Harlesden, you know, or I expect you don’t know, is quite on the out-quarters of London; something curiously different from your fine old crusted suburb like Norwood or Hampstead, different as each of these is from the other. Hampstead, I mean, is where you look for the head of your great China house with his three acres of land and pine-houses, though of late there is the artistic substratum; while Norwood is the home of the prosperous middle-class family who took the house ‘because it was near the Palace,’ and sickened of the Palace six months afterwards; but Harlesden is a place of no character. It’s too new to have any character as yet. There are the rows of red houses and the rows of white houses and the bright green Venetians, and the blistering doorways, and the little backyards they call gardens, and a few feeble shops, and then, just as you think you’re going to grasp the physiognomy of the settlement, it all melts away.”

===Chinese===

“我看你还是那个戴森，江山易改，”索尔兹伯里说，慢慢啜着基安蒂。“我看你是叫过热的想象带偏了；伦敦的神秘只存在于你的幻想里。依我看，这地方乏味得很。伦敦难得听说一起真正有艺术味的罪案；巴黎倒是遍地都是那一类。”

“再给我倒点酒。谢谢。你错了，亲爱的伙计，你实在错了。论罪案，伦敦没什么可羞愧的。我们缺的是荷马，不是阿伽门农。Carent quia vate sacro（缺的是神圣的诗人），你知道。”

“这句引文我记得。只是我不大跟得上你。”

“嗯，说白了：在伦敦，没有专攻那一类的作家。我们寻常的记者是条乏味的狗；他讲的每个故事都讲糟蹋了。他对恐怖、对什么激起恐怖的概念，贫乏得可怜。这家伙什么瞧不上，单恋血红——俗气的、红的血；弄到手就往厚里涂，自以为写出了有力量的文章。可怜的念头。而且出于某种古怪的命运，最平淡最野蛮的凶案偏偏最招眼，被写得最热闹。比如说，哈勒斯登那桩案子，你八成没听说过？”

“没有；不记得。”

“当然不。可那故事怪得很。喝咖啡的时候讲给你听。哈勒斯登，你知道——多半你不知道——在伦敦顶边远的地方；同诺伍德、汉普斯特德那种体面的老牌郊区全然两样——虽说这两处彼此也不一样。汉普斯特德，我是说，是你去寻你们大华洋行的老板的地方，三英亩地，暖房成排；虽说近来添了些艺术家底色；诺伍德则是兴旺中产人家的家——当初‘因为离水晶宫近’赁了宅子，半年后就腻了水晶宫；哈勒斯登却是个没有性格的地方。它太新，新得还没有性格。一排排红房子、一排排白房子，鲜绿的软百叶窗，起泡的门框，他们管叫花园的小后院，几家软塌塌的铺子；你正以为抓住了这聚落的相貌，它忽然整个化了。”

===Original===

“How the dickens is that? the houses don’t tumble down before one’s eyes, I suppose!”

===Chinese===

“这是怎么闹的？总不是房子当着人的眼塌了吧！”

===Original===

“Well, no, not exactly that. But Harlesden as an entity disappears. Your street turns into a quiet lane, and your staring houses into elm trees, and the back-gardens into green meadows. You pass instantly from town to country; there is no transition as in a small country town, no soft gradations of wider lawns and orchards, with houses gradually becoming less dense, but a dead stop. I believe the people who live there mostly go into the City. I have seen once or twice a laden bus bound thitherwards. But however that may be, I can’t conceive a greater loneliness in a desert at midnight than there is there at midday. It is like a city of the dead; the streets are glaring and desolate, and as you pass it suddenly strikes you that this too is part of London. Well, a year or two ago there was a doctor living there; he had set up his brass plate and his red lamp at the very end of one of those shining streets, and from the back of the house, the fields stretched away to the north. I don’t know what his reason was in settling down in such an out-of-the-way place, perhaps Dr. Black, as we will call him, was a farseeing man and looked ahead. His relations, so it appeared afterwards, had lost sight of him for many years and didn’t even know he was a doctor, much less where he lived. However, there he was settled in Harlesden, with some fragments of a practice, and an uncommonly pretty wife. People used to see them walking out together in the summer evenings soon after they came to Harlesden, and, so far as could be observed, they seemed a very affectionate couple. These walks went on through the autumn, and then ceased; but, of course, as the days grew dark and the weather cold, the lanes near Harlesden might be expected to lose many of their attractions. All through the winter nobody saw anything of Mrs. Black; the doctor used to reply to his patients’ inquiries that she was a ‘little out of sorts, would be better, no doubt, in the spring.’ But the spring came, and the summer, and no Mrs. Black appeared, and at last people began to rumour and talk amongst themselves, and all sorts of queer things were said at ‘high teas,’ which you may possibly have heard are the only form of entertainment known in such suburbs. Dr. Black began to surprise some very odd looks cast in his direction, and the practice, such as it was, fell off before his eyes.

===Chinese===

“呃，不是，倒不全是。是哈勒斯登作为一个实体消失了。你的街变成一条安静的巷子，你那些瞪眼的房子变成榆树，后花园变成绿草场。你一下子从城里到了乡下；没有小镇那种过渡，没有草坪渐宽、果园渐多、房舍渐稀的柔和层次，而是一刀齐的死断。我看住在那儿的人多半进城上工。满载的公共汽车往那边去的，我见过一两回。可不管怎样，正午时分那里的孤独感，我想象不出午夜的荒漠还能更甚。它像一座死城；街道白晃晃、荒凄凄；你走过时猛然想到：这也是伦敦的一部分。嗯，一两年前，那儿住着一位医生；他把铜牌和红灯挂在那类亮堂街道的顶头上，宅子背后，田野一直铺到北边。他为什么蹲到这么个偏远地方，我不知道；也许——咱们就叫他布莱克医生吧——是个有远见的人，往前看着呢。他的亲人们，后来才知道，多年断了音讯，连他行医都不知道，更别说住处了。反正，他在哈勒斯登落了脚，有零零星星一点诊务，还有一位出奇漂亮的太太。他们刚到哈勒斯登那阵，人们夏天傍晚常见他们一同散步；就观察所及，像是很恩爱的一对。散步延续到深秋，然后停了；当然，天短了，天冷了，哈勒斯登左近的巷子照例要失掉许多迷人处。整个冬天没人见过布莱克太太；医生对病人的探问照例答：她‘有点不适，到春天想必会好’。可春天来了，夏天也来了，布莱克太太没有露面；末了人们开始私下传话，‘高档茶会’上什么怪话都有——你或许听说过，那是这类郊区唯一知名的待客形式。布莱克医生开始吃进一些朝他甩过来的很怪的眼色；那点诊务，本来就有限，当着他的眼衰了下去。

===Original===

In short, when the neighbours whispered about the matter, they whispered that Mrs. Black was dead, and that the doctor had made away with her. But this wasn’t the case; Mrs. Black was seen alive in June. It was a Sunday afternoon, one of those few exquisite days that an English climate offers, and half London had strayed out into the fields, north, south, east, and west to smell the scent of the white May, and to see if the wild roses were yet in blossom in the hedges. I had gone out myself early in the morning, and had had a long ramble, and somehow or other as I was steering homeward I found myself in this very Harlesden we have been talking about. To be exact, I had a glass of beer in the General Gordon, the most flourishing house in the neighbourhood, and as I was wandering rather aimlessly about, I saw an uncommonly tempting gap in a hedgerow, and resolved to explore the meadow beyond. Soft grass is very grateful to the feet after the infernal grit strewn on suburban sidewalks, and after walking about for some time I thought I should like to sit down on a bank and have a smoke. While I was getting out my pouch, I looked up in the direction of the houses, and as I looked I felt my breath caught back, and my teeth began to chatter, and the stick I had in one hand snapped in two with the grip I gave it. It was as if I had had an electric current down my spine, and yet for some moment of time which seemed long, but which must have been very short, I caught myself wondering what on earth was the matter. Then I knew what had made my very heart shudder and my bones grind together in an agony. As I glanced up I had looked straight towards the last house in the row before me, and in an upper window of that house I had seen for some short fraction of a second a face. It was the face of a woman, and yet it was not human. You and I, Salisbury, have heard in our time, as we sat in our seats in church in sober English fashion, of a lust that cannot be satiated and of a fire that is unquenchable, but few of us have any notion what these words mean. I hope you never may, for as I saw that face at the window, with the blue sky above me and the warm air playing in gusts about me, I knew I had looked into another world﻿—looked through the window of a commonplace, brand-new house, and seen hell open before me.

===Chinese===

一句话，邻居们咬耳朵的时候，咬的是：布莱克太太死了，是医生把她料理掉的。可事实并非如此；六月里，布莱克太太被人见着活着。那是一个星期天下午，英国气候一年里难得赏赐的几个好日子之一；半个伦敦都散到四乡去了，闻白山植的花香，看篱边的野玫瑰开了没有。我一早也出了门，游荡了长长一天；回家路上不知怎么七拐八拐，正拐到我们说的这个哈勒斯登。说精确点，我在戈登将军酒馆——左近最兴旺的一家——喝了一杯啤酒，正漫无目的地转悠，望见树篱上一个怪诱人的缺口，便打算去那边的草场探一探。踩过郊区人行道上那种可恶的沙砾之后，软草于脚实在是恩物；走了一阵我想在土坡上坐下抽斗烟。掏烟袋的当儿，我朝房子的方向抬头一望；一望之下，气息猛地抽回，牙齿磕磕作响，手里的手杖叫我一攥，喀嚓断成两截。像一道电流顺着我脊梁滚下去；有一段工夫——觉得长，其实必是极短——我竟自纳闷到底出了什么事。随后我知道了那叫我心底发抖、骨头痛苦地磨响的缘故。我抬眼时，视线正对着我面前那排房子的最后一所；那所房子的楼上窗口，我看见了——极短的一瞬——一张脸。那是一个女人的脸，却又不是人的脸。你我，索尔兹伯里，坐在教堂座位上、以英国人的端正样子，都听过讲什么填不满的淫欲、浇不灭的火；可我们中间极少有人知道这些字是什么意思。但愿你永远不必知道；因为当我望见窗口那张脸——头顶蓝天，暖气一阵阵拂着我——我知道我望进了另一个世界：隔着一所平平常常、崭新崭新的房子的窗，看见了在我面前张开的的地狱。

===Original===

When the first shock was over, I thought once or twice that I should have fainted; my face streamed with a cold sweat, and my breath came and went in sobs, as if I had been half drowned. I managed to get up at last, and walked round to the street, and there I saw the name ‘Dr. Black’ on the post by the front gate. As fate or my luck would have it, the door opened and a man came down the steps as I passed by. I had no doubt it was the doctor himself. He was of a type rather common in London; long and thin, with a pasty face and a dull black moustache. He gave me a look as we passed each other on the pavement, and though it was merely the casual glance which one foot-passenger bestows on another, I felt convinced in my mind that here was an ugly customer to deal with. As you may imagine, I went my way a good deal puzzled and horrified too by what I had seen; for I had paid another visit to the General Gordon, and had got together a good deal of the common gossip of the place about the Blacks. I didn’t mention the fact that I had seen a woman’s face in the window; but I heard that Mrs. Black had been much admired for her beautiful golden hair, and round what had struck me with such a nameless terror, there was a mist of flowing yellow hair, as it were an aureole of glory round the visage of a satyr. The whole thing bothered me in an indescribable manner; and when I got home I tried my best to think of the impression I had received as an illusion, but it was no use. I knew very well I had seen what I have tried to describe to you, and I was morally certain that I had seen Mrs. Black. And then there was the gossip of the place, the suspicion of foul play, which I knew to be false, and my own conviction that there was some deadly mischief or other going on in that bright red house at the corner of Devon Road: how to construct a theory of a reasonable kind out of these two elements. In short, I found myself in a world of mystery; I puzzled my head over it and filled up my leisure moments by gathering together odd threads of speculation, but I never moved a step towards any real solution, and as the summer days went on the matter seemed to grow misty and indistinct, shadowing some vague terror, like a nightmare of last month.

===Chinese===

第一阵震骇过去后，我一两回想自己要晕倒；满脸淌着冷汗，呼吸一抽一抽，像呛了半死的水。末了我挣扎起来，绕到街上；前门柱上看见“布莱克医生”的名牌。也是命或运气，我走过时门开了，一个人走下台阶。我毫不怀疑那就是医生本人。他是伦敦常见的一型：又长又瘦，面团似的脸，一部死黑的胡子。我们在人行道上交错时他瞟了我一眼；虽说只是行人对行人的随意一瞥，我心里却信定了：这是个不好对付的主。你可以想见，我一路走去，又迷惑又惊骇；因为我又去了一趟戈登将军，把左近关于布莱克家的闲话搜罗了一堆。我没提我在窗口看见一张女人的脸；可我听说，布莱克太太那头金发素来为人称道；而围着我无以名状的恐怖那桩东西的，正是一蓬流泻的黄发，像山精脸上一圈荣耀的光轮。整件事说不清地缠磨我；回到家，我竭力把那印象想成错觉，没用。我知道我看见了我设法向你描述的东西；我千真万确知道那是布莱克太太。再加上那地方的闲话，那桩我知道是假的谋杀嫌疑，加上我自己的确信——德文路角上那栋鲜红房子里正进行着某种致命的祸祟：拿这两样东西，怎样搭出一套讲道理的理论？一句话，我发觉自己落在一片迷雾世界里；我为之绞脑汁，闲时就搜罗零碎的线索，可向真正的解决没挪动一步；夏日一天天过去，事情反而越来越模糊，投出一重模糊的恐怖，像上个月的噩梦。

===Original===

I suppose it would before long have faded into the background of my brain﻿—I should not have forgotten it, for such a thing could never be forgotten﻿—but one morning as I was looking over the paper my eye was caught by a heading over some two dozen lines of small type. The words I had seen were simply, ‘The Harlesden Case,’ and I knew what I was going to read. Mrs. Black was dead. Black had called in another medical man to certify as to cause of death, and something or other had aroused the strange doctor’s suspicions and there had been an inquest and postmortem. And the result? That, I will confess, did astonish me considerably; it was the triumph of the unexpected. The two doctors who made the autopsy were obliged to confess that they could not discover the faintest trace of any kind of foul play; their most exquisite tests and reagents failed to detect the presence of poison in the most infinitesimal quantity. Death, they found, had been caused by a somewhat obscure and scientifically interesting form of brain disease. The tissue of the brain and the molecules of the grey matter had undergone a most extraordinary series of changes; and the younger of the two doctors, who has some reputation, I believe, as a specialist in brain trouble, made some remarks in giving his evidence which struck me deeply at the time, though I did not then grasp their full significance. He said: ‘At the commencement of the examination I was astonished to find appearances of a character entirely new to me, notwithstanding my somewhat large experience. I need not specify these appearances at present, it will be sufficient for me to state that as I proceeded in my task I could scarcely believe that the brain before me was that of a human being at all.’ There was some surprise at this statement, as you may imagine, and the coroner asked the doctor if he meant to say that the brain resembled that of an animal. ‘No,’ he replied, ‘I should not put it in that way. Some of the appearances I noticed seemed to point in that direction, but others, and these were the more surprising, indicated a nervous organization of a wholly different character from that either of man or the lower animals.’ It was a curious thing to say, but of course the jury brought in a verdict of death from natural causes, and, so far as the public was concerned, the case came to an end.

===Chinese===

我想它不久就会退进我脑子的背景里——我不会忘掉它，这种事永远忘不掉——可一天早晨翻报，我的眼被一行标题抓住，底下两来行小字。标题就三个词：“哈勒斯登案”；我知道我要读到什么了。布莱克太太死了。布莱克请了另一位医生签署死亡证明；不知什么引起了那位陌生医生的疑心，开了验尸庭，做了解剖。结果呢？那，我承认，当真叫我吃了一惊；是意外的胜利。主持解剖的两位医生只得承认：任何谋害的痕迹，最淡的一丝也寻不见；他们最精微的试验和试剂，连最微量的毒也测不出。死亡，他们发现，由一种相当冷僻、在科学上颇有趣的脑病所致。脑组织和灰质的分子经历了一连串非同寻常的变化；两人中年轻的那位——我相信在脑病上小有专家之名——作证时说的几句话，当时深深击中我，虽然我那时还没懂得它们的全部分量。他说：‘检查之初我惊讶地发现一些我从未见过的表现，尽管我的经验不算小。这些表现眼下我不必细说；只消说：随着检查的推进，我几乎不能相信，面前这个脑子当真是一个人的。’这话自然引起一阵惊动；验尸官问他，是不是说这脑子像野兽的脑子。‘不，’他答，‘我不这样说。我注意到的一些表现似乎指向那个方向；可另一些——而且是更惊人的那些——指向一种神经组织，其性质与人的、也与低等动物的，全然不同。’这话古怪；可陪审团照例裁了自然死亡；就公众而论，案子到此为了。

===Original===

But after I had read what the doctor said I made up my mind that I should like to know a good deal more, and I set to work on what seemed likely to prove an interesting investigation. I had really a good deal of trouble, but I was successful in a measure. Though why﻿—my dear fellow, I had no notion at the time. Are you aware that we have been here nearly four hours? The waiters are staring at us. Let’s have the bill and be gone.”

The two men went out in silence, and stood a moment in the cool air, watching the hurrying traffic of Coventry Street pass before them to the accompaniment of the ringing bells of hansoms and the cries of the newsboys; the deep far murmur of London surging up ever and again from beneath these louder noises.

“It is a strange case, isn’t it?” said Dyson at length. “What do you think of it?”

“My dear fellow, I haven’t heard the end, so I will reserve my opinion. When will you give me the sequel?”

“Come to my rooms some evening; say next Thursday. Here’s the address. Good night; I want to get down to the Strand.” Dyson hailed a passing hansom, and Salisbury turned northward to walk home to his lodgings.

===Chinese===

“可读完医生的话，我打定主意要多知道一点，便动手做一番看来会有趣的调查。着实费了些力气，倒也有几分成功。至于为什么——亲爱的伙计，我当时毫不知情。你可知道，咱们在这儿坐了快四个钟头了？侍者都瞪咱们了。结账走吧。”

两人默默出门，在凉气里站了片刻，看考文垂街匆促的车流从面前过去，夹着双轮马车的铃声和报童的叫卖；伦敦深沉辽远的低响，一阵阵从这些更响的噪声底下涌上来。

“案子怪，是不是？”戴森终于说。“你怎么看？”

“亲爱的伙计，我还没听到结尾呢，意见留着。什么时候给我下文？”

“哪天晚上来我寓所；就下星期四吧。这是地址。晚安；我要下河滨去。”戴森招呼了一辆过路的双轮马车；索尔兹伯里转身往北，走回他的寓所。

### II / II

### II / 
===Original===

Mr. Salisbury, as may have been gathered from the few remarks which he had found it possible to introduce in the course of the evening, was a young gentleman of a peculiarly solid form of intellect, coy and retiring before the mysterious and the uncommon, with a constitutional dislike of paradox. During the restaurant dinner he had been forced to listen in almost absolute silence to a strange tissue of improbabilities strung together with the ingenuity of a born meddler in plots and mysteries, and it was with a feeling of weariness that he crossed Shaftesbury Avenue, and dived into the recesses of Soho, for his lodgings were in a modest neighbourhood to the north of Oxford Street. As he walked he speculated on the probable fate of Dyson, relying on literature, unbefriended by a thoughtful relative, and could not help concluding that so much subtlety united to a too vivid imagination would in all likelihood have been rewarded with a pair of sandwich-boards or a super’s banner. Absorbed in this train of thought, and admiring the perverse dexterity which could transmute the face of a sickly woman and a case of brain disease into the crude elements of romance, Salisbury strayed on through the dimly-lighted streets, not noticing the gusty wind which drove sharply round corners and whirled the stray rubbish of the pavement into the air in eddies, while black clouds gathered over the sickly yellow moon. Even a stray drop or two of rain blown into his face did not rouse him from his meditations, and it was only when with a sudden rush the storm tore down upon the street that he began to consider the expediency of finding some shelter. The rain, driven by the wind, pelted down with the violence of a thunderstorm, dashing up from the stones and hissing through the air, and soon a perfect torrent of water coursed along the kennels and accumulated in pools over the choked-up drains. The few stray passengers who had been loafing rather than walking about the street had scuttered away, like frightened rabbits, to some invisible places of refuge, and though Salisbury whistled loud and long for a hansom, no hansom appeared.

===Chinese===

索尔兹伯里先生，从他那晚插得进去的寥寥几句看，是个头脑特别敦实的年轻人：在神秘与非常之物跟前腼腆退缩，对悖论怀着天生的厌恶。餐馆的这顿饭，他被迫几乎一言不发地听了一大串未必可信的奇谈，由一个天生的情节与秘密的搅事鬼用巧劲串在一起；他带着倦意穿过沙夫茨伯里大道，钻进苏荷区的深处——他的寓所在牛津街北边一个朴素地段。他一路走，一路琢磨戴森的命运：靠文学吃饭，又没有体贴的亲戚搭救；他不由得断定，这么多的精明再加上一副太活的想象，多半只配得一副前后广告牌，或戏子里的一面大旗。他沉在这串念头里，一面叹服那股能把一张病妇的脸和一桩脑病点化成传奇原料的乖张手艺，一面在昏暗的街上信步走去，没留意一阵阵刮过街角、把人行道上的碎屑卷上半空的狂风，而病黄色的月亮上头，乌云正聚。一两滴雨吹到脸上也没把他从沉思里惊醒；直到那场风雨轰然扑下街来，他才开始考虑找个躲避处的便宜。风赶着雨，以雷雨的暴烈砸下来，从石板上溅起，在空气里嘶嘶作响；不多时，沟槽里已是急流，堵塞的下水口上积起一个个水潭。街上原本就不是走而是荡着的几个散人，早像受惊的兔子窜进了不见影子的避处；索尔兹伯里打了一声又长又响的呼哨叫车，一辆车也没有。

===Original===

He looked about him, as if to discover how far he might be from the haven of Oxford Street, but strolling carelessly along, he had turned out of his way, and found himself in an unknown region, and one to all appearance devoid even of a public-house where shelter could be bought for the modest sum of twopence. The street lamps were few and at long intervals, and burned behind grimy glasses with the sickly light of oil, and by this wavering glimmer Salisbury could make out the shadowy and vast old houses of which the street was composed. As he passed along, hurrying, and shrinking from the full sweep of the rain, he noticed the innumerable bell-handles, with names that seemed about to vanish of old age graven on brass plates beneath them, and here and there a richly carved penthouse overhung the door, blackening with the grime of fifty years. The storm seemed to grow more and more furious; he was wet through, and a new hat had become a ruin, and still Oxford Street seemed as far off as ever; it was with deep relief that the dripping man caught sight of a dark archway which seemed to promise shelter from the rain if not from the wind. Salisbury took up his position in the driest corner and looked about him; he was standing in a kind of passage contrived under part of a house, and behind him stretched a narrow footway leading between blank walls to regions unknown. He had stood there for some time, vainly endeavouring to rid himself of some of his superfluous moisture, and listening for the passing wheel of a hansom, when his attention was aroused by a loud noise coming from the direction of the passage behind, and growing louder as it drew nearer. In a couple of minutes he could make out the shrill, raucous voice of a woman, threatening and renouncing, and making the very stones echo with her accents, while now and then a man grumbled and expostulated. Though to all appearance devoid of romance, Salisbury had some relish for street rows, and was, indeed, somewhat of an amateur in the more amusing phases of drunkenness; he therefore composed himself to listen and observe with something of the air of a subscriber to grand opera. To his annoyance, however, the tempest seemed suddenly to be composed, and he could hear nothing but the impatient steps of the woman and the slow lurch of the man as they came towards him.

===Chinese===

他四下张望，想看看离牛津街这个避风港还有多远；可他一路闲荡，早拐出了正道，发觉自己到了一个陌生地段，而且看样子连一家可以花两便士买躲避的小酒馆都没有。街灯稀疏，隔老远一盏，隔着油腻的玻璃，燃着油灯病恹恹的光；借着这点摇曳的微明，索尔兹伯里辨出这条街两侧那些影影绰绰的高大老屋。他匆匆走过，缩着身子躲雨的迎头扫射，注意到数不清的门铃拉手，底下铜牌上刻着仿佛因年老而快消散的名字；这里那里，一楣雕饰富丽的雨罩探出在门上，蒙着五十年的垢腻。风雨越发狂暴；他浑身湿透，新帽子已成废墟，牛津街却像还同先前一样远；这位滴着水的人望见一道黑黑的拱廊，似乎许诺躲得开雨——风就管不了了——不由得深深松了口气。索尔兹伯里拣了最干的角落站定，四下打量：他立在一所房子底下掏出的一段过道里；身后一条窄窄的步道，夹在两堵光秃秃的墙之间，通向不知什么去处。他在那儿站了好一阵，徒劳地想把多余的水抖掉些，侧耳等着双轮马车的轮声；忽然，身后过道那头传来一阵大的响动，越近越响。两分钟后他听出一个女人又尖又哑的嗓门，威吓着、数落着，吐字震得石头嗡嗡回响；间或一个男人咕哝着辩解。索尔兹伯里虽看上去毫无浪漫可言，对街头的吵架倒有几分兴致；说实话，醉酒的几个较有趣的样相，他算得上半个行家；于是他摆好架势，带着订了包厢的歌剧迷的气派，准备听看。不料那场风暴像忽然平息了；他只听得那女人不耐的脚步和那男人迟缓的踉跄，一路朝他走来。

===Original===

Keeping back in the shadow of the wall, he could see the two drawing nearer; the man was evidently drunk, and had much ado to avoid frequent collision with the wall as he tacked across from one side to the other, like some bark beating up against a wind. The woman was looking straight in front of her, with tears streaming from her eyes, but suddenly as they went by the flame blazed up again, and she burst forth into a torrent of abuse, facing round upon her companion.

“You low rascal, you mean, contemptible cur,” she went on, after an incoherent storm of curses, “you think I’m to work and slave for you always, I suppose, while you’re after that Green Street girl and drinking every penny you’ve got? But you’re mistaken, Sam﻿—indeed, I’ll bear it no longer. Damn you, you dirty thief, I’ve done with you and your master too, so you can go your own errands, and I only hope they’ll get you into trouble.”

The woman tore at the bosom of her dress, and taking something out that looked like paper, crumpled it up and flung it away. It fell at Salisbury’s feet. She ran out and disappeared in the darkness, while the man lurched slowly into the street, grumbling indistinctly to himself in a perplexed tone of voice. Salisbury looked out after him and saw him maundering along the pavement, halting now and then and swaying indecisively, and then starting off at some fresh tangent. The sky had cleared, and white fleecy clouds were fleeting across the moon, high in the heaven. The light came and went by turns, as the clouds passed by, and, turning round as the clear, white rays shone into the passage, Salisbury saw the little ball of crumpled paper which the woman had cast down. Oddly curious to know what it might contain, he picked it up and put it in his pocket, and set out afresh on his journey.

===Chinese===

他退进墙影里，看得见两人越走越近；男人分明醉了，从这边抢到那边，像顶风抢风的小船，好容易才不撞上墙。女人直望着前方，泪水从眼里淌下来；可两人经过时，火忽然又腾起来，她回身冲着同伴，破口大骂。

“你这个下流坯，你这个卑鄙的懦货，”一阵语无伦次的咒骂之后她接着骂，“你以为我就该一直替你做牛做马，你去找绿街那骚丫头，把钱喝个精光？你打错了算盘，萨姆——真的，我再也受不了了。该死的你这脏贼，我跟你一刀两断，跟你那位老爷也一刀两断；你自己跑你的腿吧，但愿那些事把你坑进去。”

女人撕开衣襟，掏出一样像纸的东西，揉成一团摔了出去。它落在索尔兹伯里脚边。她跑了出去，没进黑暗里；男人慢慢踉跄到街上，困惑地自言自语，含混地咕哝。索尔兹伯里望着他的背影，见他沿人行道踉跄而去，走走停停，摇摇晃晃拿不定主意，忽而又朝一个新的方向蹿去。天放晴了，一团团白絮似的云掠过高天的月亮。光随云明灭；等到清白的光照进过道，索尔兹伯里转过身，看见了女人摔下的那个小纸团。想知道里头是什么的古怪好奇心占了上风；他拾起来揣进衣袋，重新上路。

### III / III

### III / 
===Original===

Salisbury was a man of habit. When he got home, drenched to the skin, his clothes hanging lank about him, and a ghastly dew besmearing his hat, his only thought was of his health, of which he took studious care. So, after changing his clothes and encasing himself in a warm dressing-gown, he proceeded to prepare a sudorific in the shape of a hot gin and water, warming the latter over one of those spirit-lamps which mitigate the austerities of the modern hermit’s life. By the time this preparation had been exhibited, and Salisbury’s disturbed feelings had been soothed by a pipe of tobacco, he was able to get into bed in a happy state of vacancy, without a thought of his adventure in the dark archway, or of the weird fancies with which Dyson had seasoned his dinner. It was the same at breakfast the next morning, for Salisbury made a point of not thinking of anything until that meal was over; but when the cup and saucer were cleared away, and the morning pipe was lit, he remembered the little ball of paper, and began fumbling in the pockets of his wet coat. He did not remember into which pocket he had put it, and as he dived now into one and now into another, he experienced a strange feeling of apprehension lest it should not be there at all, though he could not for the life of him have explained the importance he attached to what was in all probability mere rubbish. But he sighed with relief when his fingers touched the crumpled surface in an inside pocket, and he drew it out gently and laid it on the little desk by his easy-chair with as much care as if it had been some rare jewel. Salisbury sat smoking and staring at his find for a few minutes, an odd temptation to throw the thing in the fire and have done with it struggling with as odd a speculation as to its possible contents, and as to the reason why the infuriated woman should have flung a bit of paper from her with such vehemence. As might be expected, it was the latter feeling that conquered in the end, and yet it was with something like repugnance that he at last took the paper and unrolled it, and laid it out before him. It was a piece of common dirty paper, to all appearance torn out of a cheap exercise-book, and in the middle were a few lines written in a queer cramped hand.

===Chinese===

索尔兹伯里是个讲究习惯的人。回到家，浑身透湿，衣裳软塌塌挂在身上，帽子糊着一层可怕的露水；他唯一的念头是他的健康——那是他精心照管的。于是换过衣裳，裹上一件厚晨衣，他便着手调制一味发汗剂，形为热的杜松子酒加水，就着一只酒精灯温那 latter——那灯是缓和现代隐士生涯之清苦的诸般器物之一。药剂服下，一斗烟抚平了受扰的心绪，索尔兹伯里便得上床，进入一片幸福的空白，既不想黑拱廊里的遭遇，也不想戴森给晚饭下的那些怪佐料。第二天早饭也是照旧，因为索尔兹伯里有一条原则：饭没吃完，什么都不想。可杯盘一撤，晨烟一点，他想起了那个小纸团，便往湿上衣的口袋里摸。他记不得搁进了哪只口袋；一时摸这只，一时摸那只，心里起了一阵奇怪的忧惧，生怕它根本不在了——虽说那多半只是一团废物，他重视它什么，他自己死也说不上来。可指尖触到内袋里那皱巴巴的一团时，他松口气叹了出来；轻轻掏出，摊在躺椅边的小书桌上，那份小心，像捧一件稀世的珠宝。索尔兹伯里坐着抽烟，盯着他的发现望了几分钟；一股想把它扔进火里一了百了的怪诱惑，同一股同样怪的揣想——猜它的内容，猜那暴怒的女人凭什么把一片纸那样狠命摔掉——彼此相争。不出所料，末了是后者得胜；可他到底拿起那纸展开铺平在自己面前时，还带着几分憎嫌。那是一张寻常的脏纸，看样子是从一本便宜的练习簿上撕的；当中几行字，写在一手古怪局促的笔迹里。

===Original===

Salisbury bent his head and stared eagerly at it for a moment, drawing a long breath, and then fell back in his chair gazing blankly before him, till at last with a sudden revulsion he burst into a peal of laughter, so long and loud and uproarious that the landlady’s baby on the floor below awoke from sleep and echoed his mirth with hideous yells. But he laughed again and again, and took the paper up to read a second time what seemed such meaningless nonsense.

“Q. has had to go and see his friends in Paris,” it began. “Traverse Handel S. ‘Once around the grass, and twice around the lass, and thrice around the maple tree.’ ”

===Chinese===

索尔兹伯里低下头，热切地盯了它一瞬，深深吸一口气；随后仰回椅中，茫然望着前方；末了猛然一变，迸出一长串大笑，那样长、那样响、那样放肆，把楼下房东太太的娃娃从睡里惊醒，用可怕的嚎哭应和他的欢畅。他一面又一面地笑，拿起纸来再读一遍那看似全无意义的胡话。

“Q. 不得不去巴黎看他的朋友们，”它开头说。“Traverse Handel S.‘绕着草地转一圈，绕着姑娘转两圈，绕着枫树转三圈。’”

===Original===

Salisbury took up the paper and crumpled it as the angry woman had done, and aimed it at the fire. He did not throw it there, however, but tossed it carelessly into the well of the desk, and laughed again. The sheer folly of the thing offended him, and he was ashamed of his own eager speculation, as one who pores over the high-sounding announcements in the agony column of the daily paper, and finds nothing but advertisement and triviality. He walked to the window, and stared out at the languid morning life of his quarter; the maids in slatternly print dresses washing doorsteps, the fishmonger and the butcher on their rounds, and the tradesmen standing at the doors of their small shops, drooping for lack of trade and excitement. In the distance a blue haze gave some grandeur to the prospect, but the view as a whole was depressing, and would only have interested a student of the life of London, who finds something rare and choice in its very aspect. Salisbury turned away in disgust, and settled himself in the easy-chair, upholstered in a bright shade of green, and decked with yellow gimp, which was the pride and attraction of the apartments. Here he composed himself to his morning’s occupation﻿—the perusal of a novel that dealt with sport and love in a manner that suggested the collaboration of a stud-groom and a ladies’ college. In an ordinary way, however, Salisbury would have been carried on by the interest of the story up to lunchtime, but this morning he fidgeted in and out of his chair, took the book up and laid it down again, and swore at last to himself and at himself in mere irritation. In point of fact the jingle of the paper found in the archway had “got into his head,” and do what he would he could not help muttering over and over, “Once around the grass, and twice around the lass, and thrice around the maple tree.” It became a positive pain, like the foolish burden of a music-hall song, everlastingly quoted, and sung at all hours of the day and night, and treasured by the street-boys as an unfailing resource for six months together. He went out into the streets, and tried to forget his enemy in the jostling of the crowds and the roar and clatter of the traffic, but presently he would find himself stealing quietly aside, and pacing some deserted byway, vainly puzzling his brains, and trying to fix some meaning to phrases that were meaningless.

===Chinese===

索尔兹伯里拿起纸，照那怒气冲冲的女人那样把它揉皱，瞄准了火炉。可他没有扔，随手丢进书桌的井槽，又笑了。这东西的纯粹愚蠢冒犯了他；他为自己的热切揣测感到羞臊，像一个人把日报寻人启事栏里那些响亮的告白细读半天，只读到广告和琐碎。他走到窗前，望着他那个地段早晨有气无力的生活：衣衫拖沓的使女们在洗门阶，鱼贩肉贩在转他们的圈，店主们站在小店门口，为生意和热闹的缺乏而打蔫。远处一抹蓝雾给这幅景添了点气派；可整体是叫人丧气的，也只有一个伦敦生活的研究者会感兴趣——他在它的相貌里自能寻出稀罕和精采。索尔兹伯里厌恶地转过身，在躺椅里安顿下来——椅子蒙着亮绿色的面子，缀着黄绦，是他那套公寓的骄傲与招牌。他这就着手他早晨的功课：读一部小说，写的是运动与爱情，那笔路像是马夫同女子书院合作的产物。寻常日子，故事的趣味准能把索尔兹伯里一路送到午饭；可这天早晨他坐立不安，把书拿起又放下，末了光火地对自己、对自己咒骂。实情是，拱廊里那张纸的顺口溜“进了他的脑子”；随他怎样，嘴里总禁不住一遍遍念：“绕着草地转一圈，绕着姑娘转两圈，绕着枫树转三圈。”这成了一种实实在在的痛，像杂耍场一支蠢歌的副歌，被没完没了地引用，白天黑夜随时有人唱，还被街上的男孩们当半年的看家宝。他上街去，想借人潮的推搡和车马的轰响忘掉他的敌人；可不多时他就发觉自己又悄悄溜到一边，在什么无人的小路上踱来踱去，白费脑汁，想给那毫无意义的句子安一个意义。

===Original===

It was a positive relief when Thursday came, and he remembered that he had made an appointment to go and see Dyson; the flimsy reveries of the self-styled man of letters appeared entertaining when compared with this ceaseless iteration, this maze of thought from which there seemed no possibility of escape. Dyson’s abode was in one of the quietest of the quiet streets that led down from the Strand to the river, and when Salisbury passed from the narrow stairway into his friend’s room, he saw that the uncle had been beneficent indeed. The floor glowed and flamed with all the colours of the East; it was, as Dyson pompously remarked, “a sunset in a dream,” and the lamplight, the twilight of London streets, was shut out with strangely worked curtains, glittering here and there with threads of gold. In the shelves of an oak armoire stood jars and plates of old French china, and the black and white of etchings not to be found in the Haymarket or in Bond Street, stood out against the splendour of a Japanese paper. Salisbury sat down on the settle by the hearth, and sniffed the mingled fumes of incense and tobacco, wondering and dumb before all this splendour after the green rep and the oleographs, the gilt-framed mirror, and the lustres of his own apartment.

“I am glad you have come,” said Dyson. “Comfortable little room, isn’t it? But you don’t look very well, Salisbury. Nothing disagreed with you, has it?”

“No; but I have been a good deal bothered for the last few days. The fact is I had an odd kind of﻿—of﻿—adventure, I suppose I may call it, that night I saw you, and it has worried me a good deal. And the provoking part of it is that it’s the merest nonsense﻿—but, however, I will tell you all about it, by and by. You were going to let me have the rest of that odd story you began at the restaurant.”

“Yes. But I am afraid, Salisbury, you are incorrigible. You are a slave to what you call matter of fact. You know perfectly well that in your heart you think the oddness in that case is of my making, and that it is all really as plain as the police reports. However, as I have begun, I will go on. But first we will have something to drink, and you may as well light your pipe.”

Dyson went up to the oak cupboard, and drew from its depths a rotund bottle and two little glasses, quaintly gilded.

“It’s Benedictine,” he said. “You’ll have some, won’t you?”

===Chinese===

星期四到来，实在是桩解脱；他记起已同戴森约好登门；同这没完没了的重复、这仿佛无路可逃的思路迷宫一比，那位自命的文人的一套浮想倒显得有趣了。戴森的住处，在从河滨通到河边那些顶安静的街里最安静的一条；索尔兹伯里从窄楼梯进了朋友的房间，一看便知那位叔父实在仁厚。地板燃着东方的一切色彩，闪着焰；用戴森的说法，那是“梦里的落日”；伦敦街市的灯光被古怪织花的窗帘挡在外面，帘上金线处处闪光。一口橡木柜的架上立着旧法瓷的罐和盘；几张在干草市和邦德街都寻不到的蚀刻黑白画，衬在日本纸的华丽上。索尔兹伯里在炉边的长椅上坐下，嗅着熏香和烟草的混合气味；想起自家公寓的绿棱纹布和珂罗版画、涂金框的镜子和玻璃坠子，在这满屋辉煌跟前又惊又哑。

“你来了我很高兴，”戴森说。“舒服的小屋，是不是？可你气色不大好，索尔兹伯里。没吃什么不消化吧？”

“没有；只是这几天叫一件事搅得够呛。实情是，见你那晚，我碰上了一桩——一桩——奇遇，就这么叫吧；搅得我不轻。气人的是，那纯属胡扯——不过，回头我都告诉你。你正要给我讲餐馆里那桩怪事的后文呢。”

“是。可我看，索尔兹伯里，你无可救药。你是你所谓事实的奴隶。你心里明明认为那案子里的古怪全是我编的，其实它同警察报告一样明白。也罢，既然开了头，我就讲下去。不过先来点喝的，你正好点烟。”

戴森走到橡木柜前，从深处摸出一只滚圆的瓶子和两只古怪地描着金的小杯。

“本笃甜酒，”他说。“来一点，怎么样？”

===Original===

Salisbury assented, and the two men sat sipping and smoking reflectively for some minutes before Dyson began.

“Let me see,” he said at last, “we were at the inquest, weren’t we? No, we had done with that. Ah, I remember. I was telling you that on the whole I had been successful in my inquiries, investigation, or whatever you like to call it, into the matter. Wasn’t that where I left off?”

“Yes, that was it. To be precise, I think ‘though’ was the last word you said on the matter.”

“Exactly. I have been thinking it all over since the other night, and I have come to the conclusion that that ‘though’ is a very big ‘though’ indeed. Not to put too fine a point on it, I have had to confess that what I found out, or thought I found out, amounts in reality to nothing. I am as far away from the heart of the case as ever. However, I may as well tell you what I do know. You may remember my saying that I was impressed a good deal by some remarks of one of the doctors who gave evidence at the inquest. Well, I determined that my first step must be to try if I could get something more definite and intelligible out of that doctor. Somehow or other I managed to get an introduction to the man, and he gave me an appointment to come and see him. He turned out to be a pleasant, genial fellow; rather young and not in the least like the typical medical man, and he began the conference by offering me whisky and cigars. I didn’t think it worth while to beat about the bush, so I began by saying that part of his evidence at the Harlesden Inquest struck me as very peculiar, and I gave him the printed report, with the sentences in question underlined. He just glanced at the slip, and gave me a queer look. ‘It struck you as peculiar, did it?’ said he. ‘Well, you must remember that the Harlesden case was very peculiar. In fact, I think I may safely say that in some features it was unique﻿—quite unique.’ ‘Quite so,’ I replied, ‘and that’s exactly why it interests me, and why I want to know more about it. And I thought that if anybody could give me any information it would be you. What is your opinion of the matter?’

“It was a pretty downright sort of question, and my doctor looked rather taken aback.

===Chinese===

索尔兹伯里应了；两人坐着，啜着，抽着，想了一阵，戴森才开口。

“我想想，”他终于说，“咱们讲到验尸庭了，是吧？不，那已经完了。啊，记得了。我在告诉你：总的说来，我对这案子的查问、调查、随你怎么叫——算有了几分成功。我是在这儿停下的吧？”

“是，正是。说精确点，我想‘不过’是你在这件事上的最后一个词。”

“正是。那晚以后我把一切又想了一遍，得出的结论是：那个‘不过’实在是个天大的‘不过’。直说了吧，我只得承认，我查出的、或自以为查出的，实际上等于零。我离案子的心还同先前一样远。不过，我不妨把我确实知道的讲给你。你记得我说过，验尸庭上一位医生作证的几句话给我印象很深。我打定主意，第一步要设法从那位医生嘴里掏出点更确定、更可解的东西。我总算弄到一封介绍，他约我去见他。这人倒是个和气热络的角色；相当年轻，一点也不像典型的医界中人；会谈开头先敬我威士忌和雪茄。我看犯不着绕弯子，开门见山说：他在哈勒斯登验尸庭上的一部分证词我觉得非常特别，并把印好的报道递给他，有问题的句子画了线。他扫了一眼纸条，古怪地看了我一眼。‘您觉得特别，是吗？’他说。‘嗯，您得记得，哈勒斯登案本身就非常特别。说实在的，我可以稳稳当当地说：某些特征是独一无二的——独一无二。’‘正是，’我答，‘这正是它叫我感兴趣的地方，也是我想多知道一点的缘故。我想，若有人能给我消息，那就是您。您对此案的看法如何？’

“这问题够直截了当，我的医生显得有点窘。”

===Original===

“ ‘Well,’ he said, ‘as I fancy your motive in inquiring into the question must be mere curiosity, I think I may tell you my opinion with tolerable freedom. So, Mr., Mr. Dyson? if you want to know my theory, it is this: I believe that Dr. Black killed his wife.’

“ ‘But the verdict,’ I answered, ‘the verdict was given from your own evidence.’

“ ‘Quite so; the verdict was given in accordance with the evidence of my colleague and myself, and, under the circumstances, I think the jury acted very sensibly. In fact, I don’t see what else they could have done. But I stick to my opinion, mind you, and I say this also. I don’t wonder at Black’s doing what I firmly believe he did. I think he was justified.’

“ ‘Justified! How could that be?’ I asked. I was astonished, as you may imagine, at the answer I had got. The doctor wheeled round his chair and looked steadily at me for a moment before he answered.

“ ‘I suppose you are not a man of science yourself? No; then it would be of no use my going into detail. I have always been firmly opposed myself to any partnership between physiology and psychology. I believe that both are bound to suffer. No one recognizes more decidedly than I do the impassable gulf, the fathomless abyss that separates the world of consciousness from the sphere of matter. We know that every change of consciousness is accompanied by a rearrangement of the molecules in the grey matter; and that is all. What the link between them is, or why they occur together, we do not know, and most authorities believe that we never can know. Yet, I will tell you that as I did my work, the knife in my hand, I felt convinced, in spite of all theories, that what lay before me was not the brain of a dead woman﻿—not the brain of a human being at all. Of course I saw the face; but it was quite placid, devoid of all expression. It must have been a beautiful face, no doubt, but I can honestly say that I would not have looked in that face when there was life behind it for a thousand guineas, no, nor for twice that sum.’

“ ‘My dear sir,’ I said, ‘you surprise me extremely. You say that it was not the brain of a human being. What was it then?’

===Chinese===

“‘嗯，’他说，‘我料想您查问这件事的动机不过是好奇，那我不妨把我的看法相当放胆告诉您。这么说吧，戴——戴森先生是吧？您要听我的理论，就是这样：我相信布莱克医生杀了他的太太。’

“‘可裁决呢，’我答，‘裁决正是根据您自己的证词作出的。’

“‘正是；裁决依照我和我同僚的证据作出，而且照当时的情形，我认为陪审团办得很有道理。说真的，我看不出他们还能怎么办。可我的看法不改，您听着；我还要说一句：倘若布莱克真干了我坚信他干下的事，我一点不奇怪。我认为他做得对。’

“‘做得对！怎么会呢？’我问。你可以想见，这样的回答叫我惊愕。医生把椅子转过来，定定看了我一瞬，才回答。

“‘您自己不是搞科学的吧？不是；那我细讲也无用。我一向坚决反对生理学与心理学合伙。我相信两边都注定受害。没有人比我更明确地承认那道不可越的鸿沟、那无底的深渊——它隔开意识的世界与物质的世界。我们知道，意识的每一变化都伴有灰质分子的重排；知道的仅此而已。它们之间的链环是什么、为何相伴出现，我们不知道；多数权威认为我们永远不能知道。可是我要告诉您：当我做我的活计、刀在手里的时候，我不顾一切理论，确信躺在我面前的不是死去女人的脑子——根本不是人的脑子。我当然看见了那张脸；可它十分平静，毫无表情。想必曾是张漂亮的脸，无疑；可我可以凭良心说：哪怕给我一千几尼、两倍我也不干——在有生命在它后头的时候去看那张脸。’

“‘亲爱的先生，’我说，‘您叫我吃惊到极点。您说那不是人的脑子。那是什么？’”

===Original===

“ ‘The brain of a devil.’ He spoke quite coolly, and never moved a muscle. ‘The brain of a devil,’ he repeated, ‘and I have no doubt that Black found some way of putting an end to it. I don’t blame him if he did. Whatever Mrs. Black was, she was not fit to stay in this world. Will you have anything more? No? Good night, good night.’

===Chinese===

“‘一个魔鬼的脑子。’他说得十分冷静，一块肌肉也不动。‘一个魔鬼的脑子，’他重复道，‘而且我毫不怀疑，布莱克找出了个法子把它了结了。他若真做了，我不责备他。不管布莱克太太是什么，她不配留在这个世界。您还要点什么吗？不要？晚安，晚安。’”

===Original===

“It was a queer sort of opinion to get from a man of science, wasn’t it? When he was saying that he would not have looked on that face when alive for a thousand guineas, or two thousand guineas, I was thinking of the face I had seen, but I said nothing. I went again to Harlesden, and passed from one shop to another, making small purchases, and trying to find out whether there was anything about the Blacks which was not already common property, but there was very little to hear. One of the tradesmen to whom I spoke said he had known the dead woman well; she used to buy of him such quantities of grocery as were required for their small household, for they never kept a servant, but had a charwoman in occasionally, and she had not seen Mrs. Black for months before she died. According to this man Mrs. Black was ‘a nice lady,’ always kind and considerate, and so fond of her husband and he of her, as everyone thought. And yet, to put the doctor’s opinion on one side, I knew what I had seen. And then after thinking it all over, and putting one thing with another, it seemed to me that the only person likely to give me much assistance would be Black himself, and I made up my mind to find him. Of course he wasn’t to be found in Harlesden; he had left, I was told, directly after the funeral. Everything in the house had been sold, and one fine day Black got into the train with a small portmanteau, and went, nobody knew where. It was a chance if he were ever heard of again, and it was by a mere chance that I came across him at last. I was walking one day along Gray’s Inn Road, not bound for anywhere in particular, but looking about me, as usual, and holding on to my hat, for it was a gusty day in early March, and the wind was making the treetops in the Inn rock and quiver. I had come up from the Holborn end, and I had almost got to Theobald’s Road when I noticed a man walking in front of me, leaning on a stick, and to all appearance very feeble. There was something about his look that made me curious, I don’t know why, and I began to walk briskly with the idea of overtaking him, when of a sudden his hat blew off and came bounding along the pavement to my feet. Of course I rescued the hat, and gave it a glance as I went towards its owner.

===Chinese===

“从一位科学中人嘴里得来这样的意见，够怪的，是不是？他说他死也不肯在那张脸活着的时侯看它、一千几尼两千几尼也不看的时候，我想着的是我见过的那张脸；可我一个字没说。我又去了哈勒斯登，一家铺子一家铺子地买些小东西，想打听出一点关于布莱克家、还不属于公共财产的事；可听到的很少。一个同我搭话的店主说，他同那位死了的女人熟；她常在他那儿买他们那个小家用得着的杂货——他们从不雇仆人，只偶尔叫个打杂的女工——她死前好几个月他就没见过她。照这人的说法，布莱克太太是‘一位顶和气的太太’，一向周到体恤，同丈夫那样恩爱，丈夫同她也一样，人人都这么看。可是，把医生的意见放到一边，我知道我看见过什么。我把一切想过来想过去，一件对一件，看来唯一可能帮上我大忙的，只有布莱克本人；我打定主意去找他。当然，哈勒斯登找不到他；人家告诉我，葬礼一完他就走了。家里的一切都卖了；一个晴天，布莱克提着一只小提箱上了火车，去了谁也不知道的地方。再听到他的消息只能凭运气；而我末了撞见他，也全凭一桩纯粹的偶然。那天我正沿格雷律师学院路走，不为去哪儿，照例四处张望，一只手按着帽子——那是三月初一个刮风天，风把律师学院里的树顶摇得直颤。我从霍尔本那头过来，快到西奥博尔德路时，注意到前面走着一个人，拄着手杖，看样子虚弱得很。他那副模样里有一样什么叫我好奇，说不上为什么；我正加快脚步想赶上他，他的帽子忽然吹掉，一路蹦到人行道上，落到我脚边。我自然把帽子拾起，走向失主时朝它瞥了一眼。”

===Original===

It was a biography in itself; a Piccadilly maker’s name in the inside, but I don’t think a beggar would have picked it out of the gutter. Then I looked up and saw Dr. Black of Harlesden waiting for me. A queer thing, wasn’t it? But, Salisbury, what a change! When I saw Dr. Black come down the steps of his house at Harlesden he was an upright man, walking firmly with well-built limbs; a man, I should say, in the prime of his life. And now before me there crouched this wretched creature, bent and feeble, with shrunken cheeks, and hair that was whitening fast, and limbs that trembled and shook together, and misery in his eyes. He thanked me for bringing him his hat, saying, ‘I don’t think I should ever have got it, I can’t run much now. A gusty day, sir, isn’t it?’ and with this he was turning away, but by little and little I contrived to draw him into the current of conversation, and we walked together eastward. I think the man would have been glad to get rid of me; but I didn’t intend to let him go, and he stopped at last in front of a miserable house in a miserable street. It was, I verily believe, one of the most wretched quarters I have ever seen: houses that must have been sordid and hideous enough when new, that had gathered foulness with every year, and now seemed to lean and totter to their fall. ‘I live up there,’ said Black, pointing to the tiles, ‘not in the front﻿—in the back. I am very quiet there. I won’t ask you to come in now, but perhaps some other day﻿—’ I caught him up at that, and told him I should be only too glad to come and see him. He gave me an odd sort of glance, as if he were wondering what on earth I or anybody else could care about him, and I left him fumbling with his latchkey. I think you will say I did pretty well when I tell you that within a few weeks I had made myself an intimate friend of Black’s. I shall never forget the first time I went to his room; I hope I shall never see such abject, squalid misery again. The foul paper, from which all pattern or trace of a pattern had long vanished, subdued and penetrated with the grime of the evil street, was hanging in mouldering pennons from the wall. Only at the end of the room was it possible to stand upright, and the sight of the wretched bed and the odour of corruption that pervaded the place made me turn faint and sick.

===Chinese===

那帽子本身就是一部传记：里子上一家皮卡迪利帽商的字号；可我想，叫花子也不会从阴沟里捡它。随后我抬头，看见哈勒斯登的布莱克医生在等我。怪事，是不是？可是，索尔兹伯里，多大的变化！我在哈勒斯登见布莱克医生走下他家台阶时，他是个挺直的人，脚步坚实，四肢匀称；一个，我会说，正当盛年的男人。可现在，我面前蹲着这个可怜的生灵：佝偻，衰弱，两颊凹陷，头发飞快地白，四肢抖抖索索凑在一处，眼里盛着凄苦。他谢我给他捡帽子，说：‘我看我自己是捡不着的了；如今跑不动了。风真大，先生，是不是？’说完就要走；我一点一点设法把他引进话流，我们便一路往东走。我看这人巴不得甩掉我；可我不打算放他走；末了他在一条凄惨街上的一所凄惨房子前停住。那，我真相信，是我见过的最悲惨的地段之一：房子新时想必就够腌臜丑陋了，还一年年积着污秽，如今像是倾着、晃着，就要倒坍。‘我住上头，’布莱克指着屋瓦说，‘不在前头——在后头。我在那儿很清静。今天就不请您进去了，改天——’我抓住这句，告诉他我巴不得去看他。他古怪地瞥了我一眼，像是纳闷我或任何旁人到底能把他放在心上；我丢下他摸索钥匙。我想，等我告诉你不出几个星期我就成了布莱克的至交，你会说我干得不坏。我永远忘不了头一回进他房间；但愿再不见到那样的卑贱腌臜的凄惨。污秽的墙纸，花样或花样的痕迹早已褪尽，浸透了那条恶街的垢腻，成片地挂在墙上，垂着朽烂的幡。只有屋子尽头可以立直身子；那张凄惨的床和满屋的腐坏气味，看得我发晕作呕。

===Original===

Here I found him munching a piece of bread; he seemed surprised to find that I had kept my promise, but he gave me his chair and sat on the bed while we talked. I used to go to see him often, and we had long conversations together, but he never mentioned Harlesden or his wife. I fancy that he supposed me ignorant of the matter, or thought that if I had heard of it, I should never connect the respectable Dr. Black of Harlesden with a poor garreteer in the backwoods of London. He was a strange man, and as we sat together smoking, I often wondered whether he were mad or sane, for I think the wildest dreams of Paracelsus and the Rosicrucians would appear plain and sober fact compared with the theories I have heard him earnestly advance in that grimy den of his. I once ventured to hint something of the sort to him. I suggested that something he had said was in flat contradiction to all science and all experience. ‘No,’ he answered, ‘not all experience, for mine counts for something. I am no dealer in unproved theories; what I say I have proved for myself, and at a terrible cost. There is a region of knowledge which you will never know, which wise men seeing from afar off shun like the plague, as well they may, but into that region I have gone. If you knew, if you could even dream of what may be done, of what one or two men have done in this quiet world of ours, your very soul would shudder and faint within you. What you have heard from me has been but the merest husk and outer covering of true science﻿—that science which means death, and that which is more awful than death, to those who gain it. No, when men say that there are strange things in the world, they little know the awe and the terror that dwell always with them and about them.’ There was a sort of fascination about the man that drew me to him, and I was quite sorry to have to leave London for a month or two; I missed his odd talk. A few days after I came back to town I thought I would look him up, but when I gave the two rings at the bell that used to summon him, there was no answer. I rang and rang again, and was just turning to go away, when the door opened and a dirty woman asked me what I wanted. From her look I fancy she took me for a plainclothes officer after one of her lodgers, but when I inquired if Mr. Black were in, she gave me a stare of another kind. ‘There’s no Mr.

===Chinese===

我在这儿撞见他正啃一块面包；见我竟守约而来，他像是吃了一惊；可他把椅子让给我，自己坐在床沿上同我说话。我常去看他，我们有过许多长谈；可他从不提哈勒斯登，也不提他的妻子。我猜他当我对那事一无所知，或者以为，我即便听说过，也决计不会把哈勒斯登那位体面的布莱克医生同一个伦敦穷巷阁楼上的可怜人连到一处。他是个奇怪的人；我们坐着抽烟时，我常常拿不准他是疯是清醒；因为我以为，同我在他那间垢腻的窟里听他恳切陈说的那些理论相比，帕拉塞尔苏斯和玫瑰十字会最狂的梦也要算平淡而清醒的事实了。有一回我斗胆向他透了点这个意思。我说，他的某些话同一切科学、一切经验公然相悖。‘不，’他答，‘不是一切经验；我的经验也要算数。我不贩未经验证的理论；我说的，都是我自己证明过的，付出了可怕的成本。有一个知识的区域，您永远不会知道；智者远远望见便像躲瘟疫一样躲开，也难怪；可我进过那个区域。您若是知道，哪怕只是梦到，在我们这个安静的世界里可以做出什么、有一两个人已经做出过什么，您的灵魂都会在您里面战栗发晕。您从我这里听到的，不过是真正科学最外层的壳和罩——那门科学，得着它的人得到的是死，和比死更可怕的东西。不；人们说世上有些怪事的时候，他们全然不知道那始终与他们同在、绕着他们的敬畏与恐怖。’这人身上有一种吸引我的魔力；后来不得不离开伦敦一两个月，我还真舍不得他那套怪谈。回城后几天，我想去看看他；可我照旧拉了两下从前唤他的铃，没有回应。我拉了又拉，正要转身走，门开了，一个肮脏的女人问我要什么。看她的神色，我猜她把我当成了盯她哪个房客的便衣；可我一问布莱克先生在不在，她换了一副眼色瞪我。‘这儿没有布莱克

===Original===

Black lives here,’ she said. ‘He’s gone. He’s dead this six weeks. I always thought he was a bit queer in his head, or else had been and got into some trouble or other. He used to go out every morning from ten till one, and one Monday morning we heard him come in, and go into his room and shut the door, and a few minutes after, just as we was a-sitting down to our dinner, there was such a scream that I thought I should have gone right off. And then we heard a stamping, and down he came, raging and cursing most dreadful, swearing he had been robbed of something that was worth millions. And then he just dropped down in the passage, and we thought he was dead. We got him up to his room, and put him on his bed, and I just sat there and waited, while my ’usband he went for the doctor. And there was the winder wide open, and a little tin box he had lying on the floor open and empty, but of course nobody could possible have got in at the winder, and as for him having anything that was worth anything, it’s nonsense, for he was often weeks and weeks behind with his rent, and my ’usband he threatened often and often to turn him into the street, for, as he said, we’ve got a living to myke like other people﻿—and, of course, that’s true; but, somehow, I didn’t like to do it, though he was an odd kind of a man, and I fancy had been better off. And then the doctor came and looked at him, and said as he couldn’t do nothing, and that night he died as I was a-sitting by his bed; and I can tell you that, with one thing and another, we lost money by him, for the few bits of clothes as he had were worth next to nothing when they came to be sold.’ I gave the woman half a sovereign for her trouble, and went home thinking of Dr. Black and the epitaph she had made him, and wondering at his strange fancy that he had been robbed. I take it that he had very little to fear on that score, poor fellow; but I suppose that he was really mad, and died in a sudden access of his mania.

===Chinese===

先生住，’她说。‘他走了。死了六个星期了。我一向当他脑子有点邪，再不就是招过什么祸。他每天早晨十点到一点出去；有个星期一早晨，我们听见他进来，进了屋，关上门；没过几分钟，我们正坐下吃午饭，那么一声尖叫，我想我都要背过气去了。跟着听见跺脚；他冲下楼来，又骂又咒，怕死人，赌咒说有人偷了他一样值几百万的东西。随后他就往过道里一倒，我们当他死了。我们把他弄上楼，搁到床上；我就坐在那儿守着，我家那口子去请医生。窗子敞得大开，他有个小铁盒躺在地上，开着，空着；可当然啦，谁也不可能打窗子进去；至于说他有什么值钱东西，那是胡说——他的房租常常一拖几个星期，我家那口子三番五次吓唬要把他撵到街上去；他说得对，咱们跟别人一样也要挣饭吃——这话当然不假；可不知怎么，我不忍心那么办，虽说他是那么个怪人，我看从前境况还好些。后来医生来了，看了看，说没救了；当天夜里，我正坐在他床边，他就死了。我告诉您吧，七算八算，我们在他身上还赔了钱：他那几件破衣裳，卖起来简直不值什么。’我给了那女人半镑，谢她费心；一路想着布莱克医生和她给他做的这篇墓志，想着他被人偷了的那份怪念头。依我看，这一层他没什么可怕的，可怜人；多半是真疯了，死在疯病的一次骤发里。

===Original===

His landlady said that once or twice when she had had occasion to go into his room (to dun the poor wretch for his rent, most likely), he would keep her at the door for about a minute, and that when she came in she would find him putting away his tin box in the corner by the window; I suppose he had become possessed with the idea of some great treasure, and fancied himself a wealthy man in the midst of all his misery. *Explicit*, my tale is ended, and you see that though I knew Black, I know nothing of his wife or of the history of her death.﻿—That’s the Harlesden case, Salisbury, and I think it interests me all the more deeply because there does not seem the shadow of a possibility that I or anyone else will ever know more about it. What do you think of it?”

“Well, Dyson, I must say that I think you have contrived to surround the whole thing with a mystery of your own making. I go for the doctor’s solution: Black murdered his wife, being himself in all probability an undeveloped lunatic.”

“What? Do you believe, then, that this woman was something too awful, too terrible to be allowed to remain on the earth? You will remember that the doctor said it was the brain of a devil?”

“Yes, yes, but he was speaking, of course, metaphorically. It’s really quite a simple matter if you only look at it like that.”

“Ah, well, you may be right; but yet I am sure you are not. Well, well, it’s no good discussing it any more. A little more Benedictine? That’s right; try some of this tobacco. Didn’t you say that you had been bothered by something﻿—something which happened that night we dined together?”

“Yes, I have been worried, Dyson, worried a great deal. I﻿—But it’s such a trivial matter﻿—indeed, such an absurdity﻿—that I feel ashamed to trouble you with it.”

“Never mind, let’s have it, absurd or not.”

With many hesitations, and with much inward resentment of the folly of the thing, Salisbury told his tale, and repeated reluctantly the absurd intelligence and the absurder doggerel of the scrap of paper, expecting to hear Dyson burst out into a roar of laughter.

“Isn’t it too bad that I should let myself be bothered by such stuff as that?” he asked, when he had stuttered out the jingle of once, and twice, and thrice.

Dyson listened to it all gravely, even to the end, and meditated for a few minutes in silence.

===Chinese===

他的房东太太说，有一两回她有事进他房间（多半是催那可怜人的房租），他总把她堵在门口约莫一分钟；等她进去，总撞见他把铁盒往窗口的角落里收。我猜他迷上了什么大宝藏的念头，在满坑满谷的凄惨当中把自己当成了阔人。Explicit——我的故事完了；你瞧，我虽认得了布莱克，对他妻子、对她死的历史却一无所知。——这就是哈勒斯登案，索尔兹伯里；我看它叫我兴味更深，正因为看不出一丝一毫的可能——我或任何旁人——会再多知道它一分。你怎么看？”

“呃，戴森，我得说，你这是设法用你自己造的神秘把整件事围了起来。我赞成那位医生的解决：布莱克杀了他的妻子，他自己多半是个没发作透的疯子。”

“什么？这么说，你相信这女人是某种太可怕、太骇人、不该容她留在地上的东西？你记得医生的话吧：那是一个魔鬼的脑子？”

“是，是；他当然是打比方。照这么看，事情实在简单得很。”

“啊，好吧，也许你对；可我确信你不对。算了算了，再争没用。再来点本笃甜酒？这就对了；尝尝这种烟。你不是说过，咱们一起吃晚饭那晚，你被一件事搅得不行？”

“是，我是烦得够呛，戴森。我——可这事这样琐碎——实在是这样荒唐——我都害臊拿它麻烦你。”

“不妨事，说来听听，荒唐不荒唐的。”

索尔兹伯里结结巴巴，心里直怨这事的愚蠢，到底把他的故事讲了，又勉勉强强把那纸片上荒唐的消息和更荒唐的歪诗背了一遍，等着听戴森哄然大笑。

“我竟叫这样的玩意儿缠住，是不是太没出息了？”他把一遍、两遍、三遍的顺口溜磕磕绊绊说完之后问。

戴森郑重地听完了全部，听到末尾也郑重，又默默想了几分钟。

===Original===

“Yes,” he said at length, “it was a curious chance, your taking shelter in that archway just as those two went by. But I don’t know that I should call what was written on the paper nonsense; it is bizarre certainly, but I expect it has a meaning for somebody. Just repeat it again, will you, and I will write it down. Perhaps we might find a cipher of some sort, though I hardly think we shall.”

Again had the reluctant lips of Salisbury slowly to stammer out the rubbish that he abhorred, while Dyson jotted it down on a slip of paper.

“Look over it, will you?” he said, when it was done; “it may be important that I should have every word in its place. Is that all right?”

“Yes; that is an accurate copy. But I don’t think you will get much out of it. Depend upon it, it is mere nonsense, a wanton scribble. I must be going now, Dyson. No, no more; that stuff of yours is pretty strong. Good night.”

“I suppose you would like to hear from me, if I did find out anything?”

“No, not I; I don’t want to hear about the thing again. You may regard the discovery, if it is one, as your own.”

“Very well. Good night.”

===Chinese===

“是，”他终于说，“你躲进那道拱廊、正赶上那两人走过，是个古怪的巧合。可我不见得就把纸上写的叫胡话；它怪诞，不假；可我想，它对某个人有个意思。再背一遍，好吗？我记下来。说不定能找出某种密码；虽然我看不大像。”

索尔兹伯里那不情愿的嘴唇，只得又慢慢把那段他深恶的废话磕出来；戴森把它记在一张纸条上。

“核对一遍，好吗？”记完他说；“每个字各就各位也许要紧。都对？”

“对；一字不差。可我看你从中榨不出什么。你放心，那就是胡话，任性的涂鸦。我得走了，戴森。不添了；你那家伙劲儿不小。晚安。”

“要是我真查出点什么，你想听信儿吗？”

“不，不想；我不想再听这东西。那发现——若是发现——就算你自己的吧。”

“很好。晚安。”

### IV / IV

### IV / IV
===Original===

A good many hours after Salisbury had returned to the company of the green rep chairs, Dyson still sat at his desk, itself a Japanese romance, smoking many pipes, and meditating over his friend’s story. The bizarre quality of the inscription which had annoyed Salisbury was to him an attraction, and now and again he took it up and scanned thoughtfully what he had written, especially the quaint jingle at the end. It was a token, a symbol, he decided, and not a cipher, and the woman who had flung it away was in all probability entirely ignorant of its meaning; she was but the agent of the “Sam” she had abused and discarded, and he too was again the agent of someone unknown, possibly of the individual styled Q, who had been forced to visit his French friends. But what to make of “Traverse Handel S.” Here was the root and source of the enigma, and not all the tobacco of Virginia seemed likely to suggest any clue here. It seemed almost hopeless, but Dyson regarded himself as the Wellington of mysteries, and went to bed feeling assured that sooner or later he would hit upon the right track. For the next few days he was deeply engaged in his literary labours, labours which were a profound mystery even to the most intimate of his friends, who searched the railway bookstalls in vain for the result of so many hours spent at the Japanese bureau in company with strong tobacco and black tea. On this occasion Dyson confined himself to his room for four days, and it was with genuine relief that he laid down his pen and went out into the streets in quest of relaxation and fresh air. The gas-lamps were being lighted, and the fifth edition of the evening papers was being howled through the streets, and Dyson, feeling that he wanted quiet, turned away from the clamorous Strand, and began to trend away to the northwest. Soon he found himself in streets that echoed to his footsteps, and crossing a broad new thoroughfare, and verging still to the west, Dyson discovered that he had penetrated to the depths of Soho. Here again was life; rare vintages of France and Italy, at prices which seemed contemptibly small, allured the passerby; here were cheeses, vast and rich, here olive oil, and here a grove of Rabelaisian sausages; while in a neighbouring shop the whole Press of Paris appeared to be on sale.

===Chinese===

索尔兹伯里回到他那绿棱纹布椅子的怀抱许多个钟头之后，戴森还坐在他的书桌前——那桌子本身就是一部日本传奇——抽着许多斗烟，琢磨朋友的故事。那张搅扰了索尔兹伯里的字条的怪诞品质，在他却是吸引；他不时拿起来，若有所思地细看自己记下的东西，尤其末尾那段古怪的顺口溜。那是一个记号，一个象征，他断定，不是密码；摔掉它的女人多半对它的意义全然无知；她不过是她骂过又甩掉的那个“萨姆”的工具；而萨姆，又是另一个不认识的人的工具——多半就是那位不得不去访他法国朋友、人称 Q 的那位。可“Traverse Handel S.”当作何解？谜的根和源就在这里；弗吉尼亚的全部烟草怕也提示不出半条线索。看来几乎无望；可戴森自视为谜案界的威灵顿，上床时深信迟早会摸上正道。随后几天他深深埋头于文学的劳作——那劳作连他最亲密的朋友也全然莫测；他们白费劲地在铁路书摊上搜寻那许多时辰的成果——在日本写字台边、伴着烈烟与浓茶度过的时辰。这一回戴森闭门四天；撂下笔、出门寻松弛和新鲜空气时，他由衷地松快。煤气灯正在点亮；晚报的第五版正在街上一路嚎着卖；戴森觉得需要清静，从喧嚷的河滨转开，朝西北斜行。不多时他走进了只回声应着他脚步的街；穿过一条宽阔的新马路，仍偏西行，戴森发觉自己已钻进苏荷区的腹地。这儿又是一片生活：法国意大利的罕见佳酿，价钱低得近乎可鄙，向行人招手；这边是奶酪，大块而丰腴；这边是橄榄油；这边是一片拉伯雷式香肠的林子；隔壁铺子里，巴黎的全部报界仿佛都在出售。

===Original===

In the middle of the roadway a strange miscellany of nations sauntered to and fro, for there cab and hansom rarely ventured; and from window over window the inhabitants looked forth in pleased contemplation of the scene. Dyson made his way slowly along, mingling with the crowd on the cobblestones, listening to the queer babel of French and German, and Italian and English, glancing now and again at the shopwindows with their levelled batteries of bottles, and had almost gained the end of the street, when his attention was arrested by a small shop at the corner, a vivid contrast to its neighbours. It was the typical shop of the poor quarter; a shop entirely English. Here were vended tobacco and sweets, cheap pipes of clay and cherrywood; penny exercise-books and penholders jostled for precedence with comic songs, and story papers with appalling cuts showed that romance claimed its place beside the actualities of the evening paper, the bills of which fluttered at the doorway. Dyson glanced up at the name above the door, and stood by the kennel trembling, for a sharp pang, the pang of one who has made a discovery, had for a moment left him incapable of motion. The name over the shop was Travers. Dyson looked up again, this time at the corner of the wall above the lamppost, and read in white letters on a blue ground the words “Handel Street, W. C.,” and the legend was repeated in fainter letters just below. He gave a little sigh of satisfaction, and without more ado walked boldly into the shop, and stared full in the face the fat man who was sitting behind the counter. The fellow rose to his feet, and returned the stare a little curiously, and then began in stereotyped phrase﻿—

“What can I do for you, sir?”

Dyson enjoyed the situation and a dawning perplexity on the man’s face. He propped his stick carefully against the counter and leaning over it, said slowly and impressively﻿—

“Once around the grass, and twice around the lass, and thrice around the maple-tree.”

Dyson had calculated on his words producing an effect, and he was not disappointed. The vendor of miscellanies gasped, open-mouthed like a fish, and steadied himself against the counter. When he spoke, after a short interval, it was in a hoarse mutter, tremulous and unsteady.

“Would you mind saying that again, sir? I didn’t quite catch it.”

===Chinese===

马路当中，各色国民的杂拌来来往往信步走着；那儿马车和双轮车很少敢闯；一窗又一窗，住户们愉快地观赏着楼下的景象，探出身来。戴森慢慢往前蹭，混在鹅卵石上的人群里，听着法语德语、意大利语英语的怪样巴别，不时瞟一眼铺面的橱窗——一排排瞄准了行人的瓶子阵——几乎走到了街尾，一家拐角上的小铺攫住了他的注意；同左右邻居一比，它是个鲜明的对照。这是穷区的典型铺子：一家彻头彻尾的英国铺子。这儿卖烟草和糖果，黏土和樱木的贱烟斗；一便士的练习簿和笔杆同滑稽歌片挤作一堆；登着吓人插图的故事报表明，传奇在晚报的实事旁边也占有一席——晚报的招贴正在门口飘。戴森抬眼望门楣上的字号，站在水沟边发起抖来：发现者那一阵尖锐的痛，一时叫他动弹不得。铺子上方的名字是：特拉弗斯。戴森又抬头，这回望向灯柱上方的墙角，蓝底白字读出“亨德尔街，W.C.”；稍下一行，同样几个字淡一点，又写了一遍。他满意地轻轻叹了口气，二话不说大步走进铺子，直直瞪住坐在柜台后面的那个胖子。那家伙站起身，有点好奇地回瞪；然后套着老套的腔调开口——

“先生，要点什么？”

戴森享受着这局面，享受那人脸上渐渐起来的困惑。他把手杖仔细靠上柜台，探身过去，慢而重地说——

“绕着草地转一圈，绕着姑娘转两圈，绕着枫树转三圈。”

戴森算准了他的话会发生效果，他没有失望。那杂货贩子倒抽一口气，嘴张得像条鱼，扶住柜台才稳住。隔了一小会儿他才开口，声音嘶哑地咕哝，发着颤：

“先生，劳驾再说一遍行吗？我没听真。”

===Original===

“My good man, I shall most certainly do nothing of the kind. You heard what I said perfectly well. You have got a clock in your shop, I see; an admirable timekeeper, I have no doubt. Well, I give you a minute by your own clock.”

The man looked about him in a perplexed indecision, and Dyson felt that it was time to be bold.

“Look here, Travers, the time is nearly up. You have heard of Q, I think. Remember, I hold your life in my hands. Now!”

Dyson was shocked at the result of his own audacity. The man shrank and shrivelled in terror, the sweat poured down a face of ashy white, and he held up his hands before him.

“Mr. Davies, Mr. Davies, don’t say that﻿—don’t for Heaven’s sake. I didn’t know you at first, I didn’t indeed. Good God! Mr. Davies, you wouldn’t ruin me? I’ll get it in a moment.”

“You had better not lose any more time.”

The man slunk piteously out of his own shop, and went into a back parlour. Dyson heard his trembling fingers fumbling with a bunch of keys, and the creak of an opening box. He came back presently with a small package neatly tied up in brown paper in his hands, and, still full of terror, handed it to Dyson.

“I’m glad to be rid of it,” he said. “I’ll take no more jobs of this sort.”

===Chinese===

“我的好人，那种事我当然不会再做一遍。我的话你听得清清楚楚。你铺里有只钟，我看见了；一顶好的计时器，我毫不怀疑。好，按你自己的钟，给你一分钟。”

那人困惑无措地四下张望；戴森觉得，该来横的了。

“听着，特拉弗斯，时间快到了。你听说过 Q 吧，我想。记住：你的命攥在我手里。说！”

戴森被自己的大胆的战果吓了一跳。那人又缩又瘪，吓出一身；汗淌下一张死灰的脸；他双手举在胸前。

“戴维斯先生，戴维斯先生，别这么说——看老天的份上，别。我起先没认出您，真没认出。我的天！戴维斯先生，您不会毁了我吧？我这就去取。”

“你最好别再耽搁。”

那人可怜巴巴地溜出自己的铺子，进了后客厅。戴森听见他发抖的手指在一串钥匙里摸索，又听见开箱的吱呀。不多时他回来了，双手捧着一个小包，牛皮纸整整齐齐捆着；仍是满心恐惧，递给戴森。

“能脱手我真高兴，”他说。“这种差事我再也不接了。”

===Original===

Dyson took the parcel and his stick, and walked out of the shop with a nod, turning round as he passed the door. Travers had sunk into his seat, his face still white with terror, with one hand over his eyes, and Dyson speculated a good deal as he walked rapidly away as to what queer chords those could be on which he had played so roughly. He hailed the first hansom he could see and drove home, and when he had lit his hanging lamp, and laid his parcel on the table, he paused for a moment, wondering on what strange thing the lamplight would soon shine. He locked his door, and cut the strings, and unfolded the paper layer after layer, and came at last to a small wooden box, simply but solidly made. There was no lock, and Dyson had simply to raise the lid, and as he did so he drew a long breath and started back. The lamp seemed to glimmer feebly like a single candle, but the whole room blazed with light﻿—and not with light alone, but with a thousand colours, with all the glories of some painted window; and upon the walls of his room and on the familiar furniture, the glow flamed back and seemed to flow again to its source, the little wooden box. For there upon a bed of soft wool lay the most splendid jewel, a jewel such as Dyson had never dreamed of, and within it shone the blue of far skies, and the green of the sea by the shore, and the red of the ruby, and deep violet rays, and in the middle of all it seemed aflame as if a fountain of fire rose up, and fell, and rose again with sparks like stars for drops. Dyson gave a long deep sigh, and dropped into his chair, and put his hands over his eyes to think. The jewel was like an opal, but from a long experience of the shopwindows he knew there was no such thing as an opal one-quarter or one-eighth of its size. He looked at the stone again, with a feeling that was almost awe, and placed it gently on the table under the lamp, and watched the wonderful flame that shone and sparkled in its centre, and then turned to the box, curious to know whether it might contain other marvels. He lifted the bed of wool on which the opal had reclined, and saw beneath, no more jewels, but a little old pocketbook, worn and shabby with use. Dyson opened it at the first leaf, and dropped the book again appalled. He had read the name of the owner, neatly written in blue ink:

**Steven Black**, M.D.,

Oranmore,

Devon Road,

Harlesden.

===Chinese===

戴森拿了包裹和手杖，点点头走出铺子，过门时又回头望了一眼。特拉弗斯瘫坐回座位，脸还是吓得雪白，一只手掩着眼。戴森快步走开，一路着实琢磨：他这通乱弹，弹的是哪几根古怪的弦。他招呼了望见的第一辆双轮马车回家；点亮吊灯，把包裹放上桌，他停了一刻，猜灯光很快要照见的会是什么怪东西。他锁上门，割断绳子，一层一层揭开纸，末了露出一支小木匣，做工简单而结实。没有锁；戴森只消掀开盖子；一掀之下，他深深吸气，倒退一步。灯像是弱成了一支残烛；可满屋爆出光来——不止是光，是千百种颜色，是一扇彩绘窗的全部辉煌；他房间的墙上、熟悉的家具上，那辉光熊熊地映回来，仿佛又流回它的源头——那支小木匣。因为软软一层羊毛上，卧着一件最华美的宝石，戴森做梦也未见过的宝石；它里面亮着远天的蓝、岸边海水的绿、红宝石的红、深深的紫罗兰的光；而正中央像烧着，仿佛一注火的泉升起来，落下去，又升起来，火花如星，滴落如珠。戴森长长深深叹了口气，跌坐进椅子，双手掩眼，想。这宝石像一颗蛋白石；可凭他多年看橱窗的经验，他知道世上没有四分之一、八分之一这个尺寸的蛋白石。他又看那石头，怀着近乎敬畏的感觉，轻轻把它摆在灯下的桌上，望着它中央那奇妙的、闪闪烁烁的火；然后转向木匣，好奇它还装不装着别的奇迹。他掀起蛋白石卧过的那层羊毛，看见底下再没有宝石，只有一本旧的小记事本，用得又破又寒碜。戴森翻开第一页，骇然把本子又跌落。他读到了物主的名字，蓝墨水端端正正写着：

**史蒂文·布莱克**，医学博士，

奥兰莫尔，

德文路，

哈勒斯登。

===Original===

It was several minutes before Dyson could bring himself to open the book a second time; he remembered the wretched exile in his garret; and his strange talk, and the memory too of the face he had seen at the window, and of what the specialist had said, surged up in his mind, and as he held his finger on the cover, he shivered, dreading what might be written within. When at last he held it in his hand, and turned the pages, he found that the first two leaves were blank, but the third was covered with clear, minute writing, and Dyson began to read with the light of the opal flaming in his eyes.

===Chinese===

过了好几分钟，戴森才提得起劲把本子第二次打开；他想起阁楼上那个凄惨的流亡者，想起他的怪谈，也想起他在窗口见过的那张脸，和那位专家说过的话；这一齐涌上心头；手指搭着封皮，他打了个寒噤，怕里面写着的东西。末了他把它拿在手里翻页：头两页空白；第三页密密写满清秀的小字；蛋白石的光焰在他眼里烧着，戴森开始读。

### V / V

### V / 
===Original===

“Ever since I was a young man”﻿—the record began﻿—“I devoted all my leisure and a good deal of time that ought to have been given to other studies to the investigation of curious and obscure branches of knowledge. What are commonly called the pleasures of life had never any attractions for me, and I lived alone in London, avoiding my fellow-students, and in my turn avoided by them as a man self-absorbed and unsympathetic. So long as I could gratify my desire of knowledge of a peculiar kind, knowledge of which the very existence is a profound secret to most men, I was intensely happy, and I have often spent whole nights sitting in the darkness of my room, and thinking of the strange world on the brink of which I trod. My professional studies, however, and the necessity of obtaining a degree, for some time forced my more obscure employment into the background, and soon after I had qualified I met Agnes, who became my wife. We took a new house in this remote suburb, and I began the regular routine of a sober practice, and for some months lived happily enough, sharing in the life about me, and only thinking at odd intervals of that occult science which had once fascinated my whole being. I had learnt enough of the paths I had begun to tread to know that they were beyond all expression difficult and dangerous, that to persevere meant in all probability the wreck of a life, and that they led to regions so terrible, that the mind of man shrinks appalled at the very thought. Moreover, the quiet and the peace I had enjoyed since my marriage had wiled me away to a great extent from places where I knew no peace could dwell. But suddenly﻿—I think indeed it was the work of a single night, as I lay awake on my bed gazing into the darkness﻿—suddenly, I say, the old desire, the former longing, returned, and returned with a force that had been intensified ten times by its absence; and when the day dawned and I looked out of the window, and saw with haggard eyes the sunrise in the east, I knew that my doom had been pronounced; that as I had gone far, so now I must go farther with unfaltering steps. I turned to the bed where my wife was sleeping peacefully, and lay down again, weeping bitter tears, for the sun had set on our happy life and had risen with a dawn of terror to us both.

===Chinese===

“我还年轻的时候起”——自述这样开头——“就把全部闲暇、还有许多本该给别门功课的时间，献给冷僻幽暗的知识的探究。寻常所谓人生的行乐，于我素来无味；我独自住在伦敦，避开同学们，也被他们避开，当作一个只顾自己不通人情的家伙。只要能满足我对一种特殊知识的渴望——其存在本身对多数人便是深藏的秘密——我便极度快活；我常常整夜坐在屋里的黑暗中，想着我脚边那个奇异世界的边缘。然而我的专业功课和考取学位的必要，一度把我那门幽暗的营生挤到背后；取得资格后不久，我遇见了阿格尼丝，她成了我的妻子。我们在这遥远的郊区赁了新居，我开始清醒诊务的寻常日课；有几个月过得相当快乐，参与着四周的生活，只是偶尔想起那门曾迷住我整个存在的隐秘科学。我起步走的那几条路，我已经学得够多，知道它们难以形容地艰险；坚持下去多半意味着一生的沉毁；它们通向的地界那样可怕，人的念头一碰就吓得缩回。何况婚后享有的安静与平安，已在很大程度上把我从那些我知道绝无平安的处所诱开。可是忽然——我确实想，那是一夜之间的事，我睁着眼躺在床上，望着黑暗——忽然，我说，旧的欲望、从前的渴望回来了，而且因了这番缺席，回来时猛了十倍；天亮时我望向窗外，用枯槁的眼看东方的日出，我知道我的判决下了；我既已走得远，如今就必须以不踌躇的步子走得更远。我转向妻子安睡的床，重新躺下，流着苦泪；太阳已落在我们的幸福生活上，又带着一个恐怖的黎明，为我们两个升起来。

===Original===

I will not set down here in minute detail what followed; outwardly I went about the day’s labour as before, saying nothing to my wife. But she soon saw that I had changed; I spent my spare time in a room which I had fitted up as a laboratory, and often I crept upstairs in the grey dawn of the morning, when the light of many lamps still glowed over London; and each night I had stolen a step nearer to that great abyss which I was to bridge over, the gulf between the world of consciousness and the world of matter. My experiments were many and complicated in their nature, and it was some months before I realized whither they all pointed, and when this was borne in upon me in a moment’s time, I felt my face whiten and my heart still within me. But the power to draw back, the power to stand before the doors that now opened wide before me and not to enter in, had long ago been absent; the way was closed, and I could only pass onward. My position was as utterly hopeless as that of the prisoner in an utter dungeon, whose only light is that of the dungeon above him; the doors were shut and escape was impossible. Experiment after experiment gave the same result, and I knew, and shrank even as the thought passed through my mind, that in the work I had to do there must be elements which no laboratory could furnish, which no scales could ever measure. In that work, from which even I doubted to escape with life, life itself must enter; from some human being there must be drawn that essence which men call the soul, and in its place (for in the scheme of the world there is no vacant chamber)﻿—in its place would enter in what the lips can hardly utter, what the mind cannot conceive without a horror more awful than the horror of death itself. And when I knew this, I knew also on whom this fate would fall; I looked into my wife’s eyes. Even at that hour, if I had gone out and taken a rope and hanged myself, I might have escaped, and she also, but in no other way. At last I told her all. She shuddered, and wept, and called on her dead mother for help, and asked me if I had no mercy, and I could only sigh. I concealed nothing from her; I told her what she would become, and what would enter in where her life had been; I told her of all the shame and of all the horror.

===Chinese===

此后的事，我不在此细录；表面上我照旧操持白日的营生，对妻子一字不提。可她不久就看出我变了；我把余暇都耗在一间布置成实验室的屋子里；常常，在灰蒙蒙的清晨，当万家灯火还亮在伦敦上空，我蹑手蹑脚上楼；每夜我向那道我必须跨过的深渊——意识的世界与物质的世界之间的鸿沟——又偷近一步。我的实验繁多而复杂；过了几个月我才明白它们全都指向哪里；当这一点在一瞬间灌进我心，我觉得脸白了，心停了。可退回的力量，站在那扇此刻向我敞开的门前而不进去的力量，早已不存在；路已封死，我只能向前。我的处境同一个黑牢囚徒一样绝望无门——他唯一的光是头顶上一层牢房的光；门都关了，逃脱无望。一次又一次的实验给出同一个结果；我知道了——念头掠过心里时我自己也缩了一下——在我必须做的这件活计里，必得有实验室供不起、天平称不出的元素。在这件连我都怀疑能否活着脱身的活计里，必须进入生命本身；必须从某个活人身上抽出人们称作灵魂的那种精粹；而它的位置上（因为世界的格局里没有空着的房间）——它的位置上会进来嘴唇几乎说不出、头脑一想便觉比死本身更可怕的恐怖。知道了这一点，我也就知道了这命运会落在谁身上；我望进我妻子的眼睛。哪怕在那一刻，我若出门寻一根绳子上吊，我还能逃脱，她也一样；再没有别的路。末了我对她全说了。她战栗，流泪，唤她死去的母亲来救，问我有没有一点慈悲；我只能叹气。我什么都没有瞒她；我告诉她她将变成什么，她的生命离开之后会进来什么；我把一切耻辱、一切恐怖都告诉了她。”

===Original===

You who will read this when I am dead﻿—if indeed I allow this record to survive﻿—you who have opened the box and have seen what lies there, if you could understand what lies hidden in that opal! For one night my wife consented to what I asked of her, consented with the tears running down her beautiful face, and hot shame flushing red over her neck and breast, consented to undergo this for me. I threw open the window, and we looked together at the sky and the dark earth for the last time; it was a fine starlight night, and there was a pleasant breeze blowing, and I kissed her on her lips, and her tears ran down upon my face. That night she came down to my laboratory, and there, with shutters bolted and barred down, with curtains drawn thick and close, so that the very stars might be shut out from the sight of that room, while the crucible hissed and boiled over the lamp, I did what had to be done, and led out what was no longer a woman. But on the table the opal flamed and sparkled with such light as no eyes of man have ever gazed on, and the rays of the flame that was within it flashed and glittered, and shone even to my heart. My wife had only asked one thing of me; that when there came at last what I had told her, I would kill her. I have kept that promise.”

---

There was nothing more. Dyson let the little pocketbook fall, and turned and looked again at the opal with its flaming inmost light, and then with unutterable irresistible horror surging up in his heart, grasped the jewel, and flung it on the ground, and trampled it beneath his heel. His face was white with terror as he turned away, and for a moment stood sick and trembling, and then with a start he leapt across the room and steadied himself against the door. There was an angry hiss, as of steam escaping under great pressure, and as he gazed, motionless, a volume of heavy yellow smoke was slowly issuing from the very centre of the jewel, and wreathing itself in snakelike coils above it. And then a thin white flame burst forth from the smoke, and shot up into the air and vanished; and on the ground there lay a thing like a cinder, black and crumbling to the touch.

===Chinese===

“你——在我死后读到这些的人（倘若我竟容这册子留存）——你——打开木匣、看见了里面躺着什么的人：但愿你能懂得那颗蛋白石里藏着什么！因为有一夜，我的妻子应允了我求她的事；应允时泪水流下她美丽的脸，滚烫的羞红烧过她的颈和胸；她应允为我受这一遭。我推开窗，我们最后望了一眼天空和黑黑的大地；那是个晴好的星夜，一阵宜人的微风；我吻她的唇，她的泪落在我的脸上。那夜她下到我的实验室；在那儿，百叶闩死窗闩牢，厚帘拉得严丝合缝，连星光也不许进那屋子的眼——坩埚在灯上嘶嘶滚沸——我做下了必须做的事，领出来的，已不再是一个女人。而桌上，蛋白石燃着、闪着人眼从未见过的光；它里面那团火的芒闪烁着、放着亮，一直照进我的心。我妻子只求过我一件事：当那件事终于来到时——我对她说过的那件——我要杀了她。这个诺言，我守住了。”

---

再没有别的了。戴森任那本小记事本落下，转身又看那颗蛋白石，看它至内燃烧的光；随后，一股不可抗拒的无从言说的恐怖涌上心头，他一把攥住宝石，摔在地上，用脚跟碾。转过身时他的脸白得怕人；有一刻，他恶心地发着抖立着；随即一惊，纵身跃过房间，扶住门稳住。只听一声怒嘶，像高压下逃逸的蒸汽；他一动不动地望着：一股浓重的黄烟正从宝石的正中央缓缓冒出，在它上方盘成蛇一样的圈。随后一道细白的火从烟里迸出，射上空中，没了；地上躺着一样东西，像一块焦炭，黑的，一碰即碎。
